Global regularity and decay behavior for Leray equations with critical-dissipation and Its Application to Self-similar Solutions
Changxing Miao, Xiaoxin Zheng

Abstract
. In this paper, we show the global regularity and the optimal decay of weak solutions to the generalized Leray problem with critical dissipation. Our method is based on the maximal smoothing effect, L p L^{p} -type elliptic regularity of linearization, and the action of the heat semigroup generated by the fractional powers of Laplace operator on distributions with Fourier transforms supported in an annulus. As a by-product, we shall construct a self-similar solution to the three-dimensional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, and more importantly, prove the global regularity and the optimal decay without additional requirement of existing literatures.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q30; 35B40; 76D05.

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we study the regularity and decay behavior of weak solutions to the generalized Leray problem in
\mathbf{R}^{3}
governed by

(1.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{U}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{U}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}+\nabla P+\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F},\\
&\mbox{div}\,\mathbf{U}=0.\\
\end{aligned}\ \right.

It arises from studying the forward self-similar solutions of Cauchy problem to the three-dimensional incompressible fractional Navier-Stokes equations with
\alpha\in(0,1]
, which reads as follows

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{u}_{t}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}+(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}+\nabla\pi=0,\\
&\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

complemented with the initial condition

(1.3)

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},0)=\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{in}\ \ \ \mathbf{R}^{3}.

When
0<\alpha<1
, the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha}
is also called the Lévy operator

(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})=c_{\alpha}{\text{p.v.}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{y})}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+2\alpha}}\;{\rm d}\mathbf{y},\;\;\;c_{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha(1-\alpha)4^{\alpha}\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}+\alpha)}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}}.

To illustrate the self-similar solutions to the system (
1.2
) firstly raised by Leray in
[
12
]
, let us review the well-known scaling property, that is, if
(\mathbf{u},\pi)
is a solution to the system (
1.2
), then so is
(\mathbf{u}_{\lambda},\pi_{\lambda})
for each
\lambda>0,
where

\mathbf{u}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},t)=\lambda^{2\alpha-1}\mathbf{u}\big(\lambda\mathbf{x},\lambda^{2}t\big)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \pi_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},t)=\lambda^{2(2\alpha-1)}\pi\big(\lambda\mathbf{x},\lambda^{2}t\big).

It has been shown that the scaling invariance plays an essential role in the regularity theory of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. This scaling indicates that the ratio
\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{t}
is important and suggests that we search for a special solution which is scale invariant with respect to the natural scaling,

\mathbf{u}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{u}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{\lambda}=\pi\quad\text{for each}\quad\lambda>0,

which are often called self-similar solutions. Taking
\lambda(t)=t^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
with
t>0
(or
\lambda(t)=(-t)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
with
t<0
),
we obtain the forward self-similar solutions (or backward self-similar solutions, resp.) to the system (
1.2
) having the form

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)=\lambda^{2\alpha-1}(t)\mathbf{u}\big(\lambda(t)\mathbf{x},1\big)\quad\text{and}\ \ \ \pi(\mathbf{x},t)=\lambda^{2(2\alpha-1)}(t)\pi\big(\lambda(t)\mathbf{x},1\big).

Denoting
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},1)
and
P(\mathbf{x})=\pi(\mathbf{x},1)
, one easily verifies that the profile pair
(\mathbf{U},P)
solves either the generalized Leray equations in
\mathbf{R}^{3}

(1.4)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{U}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{U}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}+\nabla P+\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}=0,\\
&\operatorname{div}\,\mathbf{U}=0,\\
\end{aligned}\ \right.

or

(1.5)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{U}+\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{U}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}+\nabla P+\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}=0,\\
&\operatorname{div}\,\mathbf{U}=0,\\
\end{aligned}\ \right.

Existence results for the backward self-similar solutions to the system (
1.5
) were obtained in, see, e.g.,
[
15
,
19
]
. In this paper, we are interested in the forward self-similar solutions to the problem (
1.4
). Due to the singularity arising from self-similarity, we cannot directly construct a solution in the Sobolev space. Thus, one decomposes

\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}_{0}+\mathbf{V}

with
\mathbf{U}_{0}(\mathbf{x})=e^{-(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})
and the difference
\mathbf{V}
satisfies in
\mathbf{R}^{3}

(1.6)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{V}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}+\nabla P=-\mathbf{V}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}+L_{\mathbf{U}_{0}}(\mathbf{V})+\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F}_{0},\\
&\operatorname{div}\,\mathbf{V}=0,\end{aligned}\ \right.

where
\mathbf{F}_{0}=-\mathbf{U}_{0}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{0}
and

L_{\mathbf{U}_{0}}\mathbf{V}=-\mathbf{U}_{0}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{U}_{0}.

For such system with
\alpha=1
, existence of weak solutions
\mathbf{V}\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
was firstly shown by Korobkov and Tsai in
[
9
]
. But they said they did not prove regularity and decay estimate for the weak solutions.
This problem was later solved in the following theorem by Lai, Miao and Zheng in
[
10
]
.

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem 3.6,
[
10
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{5}{8},1]
and
\mathbf{u}_{0}=\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|^{2\alpha-1}}
with
\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(\mathbf{x}/|\mathbf{x}|)\in C^{1,0}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Then there exists
C=C\big(\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\big)>0
such that the system (
3.1
) admits at least one weak solution
\mathbf{V}\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
with

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\big(\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\big).

In particular, for
\alpha=1
,

|\mathbf{V}|(\mathbf{x})\leq C\big(\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\big)(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{-3}\log(e+|\mathbf{x}|).

In Theorem
1.1
, it is also proved that the existence of weak solutions
\mathbf{V}\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
to the system (
3.1
) with
\alpha\in(\frac{5}{8},1)
by using the blowup argument and Leray’s degree theory. Recently, we further develop some estimates in the weighted framework, and then we obtain the regularity and decay estimate of weak solutions for the case where
\alpha\in(\frac{5}{6},1)
, but it is not optimal.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
11
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{5}{6},1]
and
\mathbf{u}_{0}=\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|^{2\alpha-1}}
with
\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(\mathbf{x}/|\mathbf{x}|)\in C^{2,0}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Assume that
\mathbf{V}\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
is the weak solution established in Theorem
1.1
.
Then we have that
\mathbf{V}\in H^{1+\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
satisfies

\big\|\sqrt{1+|\cdot|}\mathbf{V}\big\|_{H^{1+\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\big(\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\big).

Moreover, one has

|\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{x})|\leq C\big(\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\big)(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Remark 1.3
.

Now we try to explain why
\alpha>5/6
by the scaling analysis, namely, if
\mathbf{V}
is the solution to the system (
1.6
), then so does
\mathbf{V}_{\lambda}
for each
\lambda>0,
where

\mathbf{V}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\lambda^{2\alpha-1}\mathbf{V}(\lambda\mathbf{x}).

A direct calculation yields

\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\lambda}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbf{R}^{3}\right)}=\lambda^{2\alpha-1+\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbf{R}^{3}\right)}=\lambda^{3\alpha-\frac{5}{2}}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbf{R}^{3}\right)},

which implies
\alpha=\frac{5}{6}
is “the critical index”. Thus the problem (
1.6
) is classified as “critical” in the fractional powers of Laplace operator
\alpha=\frac{5}{6}
.

Therefore, the goal of the present paper is to study the global regularity and decay behaviour of weak solution to the generalized Leray problem with critical dissipation. To do this, it makes us think of a very similar problem, the stationary solutions to the four-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations in
\Omega\subset\mathbf{R}^{4}

(1.7)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\Delta\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{V}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}+\nabla P=\mathbf{F},\\
&\operatorname{div}\,\mathbf{V}=0.\end{aligned}\right.

In 1979, Gerhardt
[
6
]
proved the regularity of weak solutions to the system (
1.7
) and his proof strongly relies on two ingredients.
One is the so-called “compactness lemma” that if
\mathbf{U}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{V}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, we have

\int_{\Omega}|\mathbf{U}|\cdot|\mathbf{V}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\varepsilon\|\mathbf{V}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}+C_{\varepsilon}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\quad\text{for each}\,\,\varepsilon>0.

Another is
L^{p}
-theory of Stokes equations, which is the linear part of the model (
1.7
), that if
\mathbf{V}
satisfies the Dirichlet problem of Stokes equations

(1.8)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\mathbf{V}+\nabla P=&\mathbf{F},\quad\mathbf{x}\in\Omega,\\
\mathbf{V}=&0,\quad\,\mathbf{x}\in\partial\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}\,\mathbf{V}=&0,\quad\,\mathbf{x}\in\Omega,\end{aligned}\ \right.

we have by using bounds for Calderón-Zygmund integral singular operators
[
16
]
that

(1.9)

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{W^{2,p}(\Omega)}+\|P\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\leq C(p,\Omega)\|\mathbf{F}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\quad\text{for each}\,\,p\in(1,+\infty).

Based on the mathematical similarity between (
1.1
) and (
1.8
), we would like to borrow this idea to apply to the weak solutions to the Leray problem (
1.1
) to get global regularity, but it does not work. The first difficulty we meet is that
L^{p}
elliptic regularity is no longer available for solution of linearization

(1.10)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{V}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}+\nabla P=\mathbf{F},\\
&\mbox{div}\,\mathbf{V}=0,\\
\end{aligned}\ \right.

due to the coefficient
\mathbf{x}
is unbounded over whole space
\mathbf{R}^{3}
. Indeed, if one estimates the solution
\mathbf{V}
to the system (
1.10
) directly by making use of (
1.9
) for each
p\in(1,+\infty),

(1.11)

\|\mathbf{V}\|_{W^{2\alpha,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\|P\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C(p,\alpha)\left(\|\mathbf{F}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\|\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\|\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right),

it is not clear how to control the last term. Hence, to proceed further, we need to look for new methods to obtain
W^{2\alpha,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
-regularity for weak solutions to the problem (
1.10
). Viewed from the perspective of microlocal analysis, it can be thought of as the maximal smoothing effect in the
L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
framework. This observation suggests us to consider localization of solution in frequency space. Performing
\dot{\Delta}_{q}
to both sides of the first equation of (
1.10
), we easily find that the couple
(\mathbf{V}_{q},P_{q}):=(\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbf{V},\dot{\Delta}_{q}P)
is smooth and solves the following system in
\mathbf{R}^{3}

(1.12)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{q}-\frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{q}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}_{q}+\nabla P_{q}=\mathbf{F}_{q}+\mathbf{R}_{q},\\
&\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where the commutator
[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}
is defined by

[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}=\dot{\Delta}_{q}\left(\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{V}\right)-\mathbf{x}\otimes\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbf{V}.

Next, taking the standard
L^{p}
-estimate of
\mathbf{V}_{q}
with
p\geq 2
, one has

(1.13)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p}\\
&-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
=&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(-\nabla P_{q}+\mathbf{F}_{q}\right)\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{R}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

Formally, we will meet a new difficulty caused by the commutator, excluding the difficulty arising from
\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}
. In order to overcome this new difficulty, we see that commutator can be rewritten as

\displaystyle[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\mathbf{y}\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\otimes\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

Taking Fourier transform of commutator and using the following property of Fourier transform which is the key point to overcome the first difficulty

(\mathbf{x}\cdot f)^{\wedge}(\xi)=i\operatorname{div}_{\xi}\hat{f}(\xi),

we immediately get

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left([\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}\right)(\xi)=i2^{-q}\nabla_{\xi}\varphi\left(2^{-q}{\xi}\right)\otimes\widehat{\mathbf{V}}(\xi)=i2^{-q}\nabla_{\xi}\varphi\left({2^{-q}{\xi}}\right)\otimes\widehat{\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}}(\xi),

which means that

[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}=2^{-q}\bar{\varphi}_{q}\ast\left(\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right)(\mathbf{x})\quad\text{with}\quad\bar{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{R}^{3}).

This equality enables us to bound the commutator as follows

(1.14)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{R}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\frac{C}{2\alpha}\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p-1}.

Next, we deal with integral term involving
\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}
. Integrating by parts, it can bounded as follows

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2p\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2p\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

It is surely a basic point that we employ the localization method so as to make this happen. This can help us to make best use of the available structure of
\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}
in the equations (
1.10
), not as perturbation in (
1.11
).

Inserting the estimate (
1.14
) and the following lower bound concerning dissipation from the Bernstein inequality in Lemma
2.9

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq c_{p}2^{2q\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})},\quad\forall p\in[2,+\infty)

into the equality (
1.13
) leads to

(1.15)

c_{p}2^{2q\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\left\|\nabla P_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\mathbf{F}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+C\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Since

-\Delta P_{q}=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F}_{q}\quad\text{in }\mathbf{R}^{3},

we have the classical elliptic estimate that for each
p\in[2,\infty),

\left\|P_{q}\right\|_{\dot{W}^{2,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F}_{q}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

This estimate together with the estimate (
1.15
) yields the
L^{p}
-type elliptic regularity that for each
p\in[2,+\infty),

(1.16)

\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{2\alpha}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|P\right\|_{\dot{B}^{1}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C_{p,\alpha}\left(\left\|\mathbf{F}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right).

In addition, with the help of Bony’s paraproduct decomposition, we develop a Besov type compactness lemma: for the divergence free vector field
\mathbf{U}\in\dot{H}^{\frac{5}{6}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
and
\mathbf{V}\in{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
with
2\leq p<\frac{9}{2}
, we have

(1.17)

\|\operatorname{div}\left(\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{V}\right)\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\varepsilon\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+C_{\varepsilon,\,p,\,\mathbf{U}}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\quad\text{for each}\,\,\varepsilon>0.

With both estimates (
1.16
) and (
1.17
) in hand, we establish our first results stated as follows:

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
\mathbf{F}\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
.
Then the problem (
1.1
) admits at least one weak solution
(\mathbf{U},P)\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times L^{\frac{3}{3\alpha-2}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
such that

(i)

for each the divergence-free vector field
{\phi}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
,

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{U}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\frac{2-\alpha}{\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{U}\cdot\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{y}\cdot\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{U}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\\
=&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}P\operatorname{div}\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{F}:\nabla\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y};\end{split}

(ii)

when
p\in[5/6,1],
we have
(\mathbf{U},P)\in H^{2\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
. Moreover,

(1)

if
\mathbf{F}\in\dot{B}_{p,p}^{s-1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
with
s\geq 0
and
p\in(2,+\infty),
we have

(\mathbf{U},P)\in B_{p,p}^{s+2\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times B_{p,p}^{s+1}(\mathbf{R}^{3}).

(2)

if
\mathbf{F}\in H_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}^{s+1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
with
s\geq 0
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
,
we have

(\mathbf{U},P)\in H^{s+\alpha}_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times H_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}^{s+\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3}).

(3)

if
\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left||\mathbf{x}|^{4\alpha-2}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{x})\right|+\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left||\mathbf{x}|^{4\alpha-1}\nabla\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{x})\right|<+\infty,
we have

(1.18)

|\mathbf{U}|(\mathbf{x})\leq C\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{-(4\alpha-1)}\log\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle\ \ \text{for each}\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}.

Moreover, if there exists some
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that

\left\||\cdot|^{4\alpha-1+\gamma}\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F}\right\|_{\dot{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}<\infty,

we have

(1.19)

|\mathbf{U}|(\mathbf{x})\leq C\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{-(4\alpha-1)}\ \ \text{for each}\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}.

Remark 1.5
.

The existence of weak solutions can be obtained by performing the same argument such as used in
[
11
,
19
]
, so we omit it and mainly focus on the global regularity and the optimal decay estimate through our paper.

Remark 1.6
.

In the proof of the decay estimate (
1.18
), we firstly find that the solution
\mathbf{U}
can be expressed by

(1.20)

\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{0}^{1}e^{-(-\Delta)^{\alpha}(1-s)}\mathbb{P}{\rm div}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(s^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-2}\mathbf{G}\big(\cdot/s^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\big)\right)\,{\rm d}s,

where
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}+\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbb{P}
is the Leray projector. Secondly, we develop the action of the heat semigroup generated by the fractional powers of Laplace operator on distributions with Fourier transforms supported in an annulus

(1.21)

\left\|\big(|\cdot|^{m}D^{\gamma}\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbb{P}G_{\alpha}\big)\ast f\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C2^{q(|\gamma|-m)}e^{-ct2^{2q\alpha}}\big\|f\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Remark 1.7
.

Without the additional assumptions, the logarithmic loss in decay estimate (
1.18
) is caused by Leray projector
\mathbb{P},
which seems inevitable.

Similar to the problem (
1.1
), we can show the global regularity and decay behaviour for weak solutions to the problem (
1.6
) following from Theorem
1.4
that

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\alpha\in[\frac{5}{6},1]
and
\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|^{2\alpha-1}}
with
\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(\mathbf{x}/|\mathbf{x}|)\in C^{1,0}(\mathbb{S}^{2}).
Assume that
(\mathbf{V},P)\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times L^{\frac{3}{3-2\alpha}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
is a weak solution to the problem (
1.6
) constructed in Theorem
1.1
. Then we have that for each
s\geq 0
and
p\in(2,+\infty),

(\mathbf{V},P)\in\left(B_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times B_{p,p}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\right)\bigcap\big(H^{s}_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times H_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\big).

More importantly, we have the optimal decay estimate

(1.22)

|\mathbf{V}|(\mathbf{x})\leq C\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{-(4\alpha-1)}\ \ \,\,\text{for each}\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}.

Remark 1.9
.

Compared with the force in (
1.1
), the problem (
1.6
) has the very special force
\operatorname{div}(\mathbf{U}_{0}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{0})
with
\mathbf{U}_{0}(\mathbf{x})=e^{-(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})
. This symmetric structure and the action of the heat semigroup (
1.21
) can help us to obtain the optimal decay estimate (
1.22
) without logarithmic loss.

Since the forward self-similar solution
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)
takes the form

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)={{t}^{-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}}\mathbf{U}\big(\mathbf{x}/{t}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\big)={{t}^{-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}}\left(\mathbf{V}+e^{\Delta}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)\big(\mathbf{x}/{t}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\big),

we immediately get by using Theorem
1.8
that

Corollary 1.10
.

Let
\alpha\in[\frac{5}{6},1]
and
\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{x})}{|\mathbf{x}|^{2\alpha-1}}
with
\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(\mathbf{x}/|\mathbf{x}|)\in C^{1,0}(\mathbb{S}^{2}).
Assume that the
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{BC}_{\rm w}\big([0,+\infty),\,L^{\frac{3}{2\alpha-1},\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\big)
is the forward self-similar solution to Cauchy problem (
1.2
)-(
1.3
) established in
[
10
]
. Then

(1)

For
\forall p\in[2,+\infty)
and
\forall s\in[0,+\infty)
, we have that

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)-e^{-t(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})

and
\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N},

\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\big\|D^{\alpha}\left(\mathbf{u}(t)-e^{-t(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)\big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq Ct^{\frac{2\alpha+k-1}{2\alpha}},

which implies that the
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)
is smooth in
\mathbf{R}^{3}\times(0,+\infty);

(2)

we have the following pointwise estimates

(1.23)

|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)|\leq\frac{C}{|\mathbf{x}|^{2\alpha-1}+{t}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)-e^{t\Delta}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right|\leq\frac{C}{|\mathbf{x}|^{4\alpha-1}+t^{\frac{4\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}}

for all
(\mathbf{x},t)\in\mathbf{R}^{3}\times(0,+\infty)
.

Remark 1.11
.

(i)

For the subcritical case
\alpha\in(5/6,1]
, we here establish the global regularity

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)-e^{-t(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})

for each
s\geq 0
and
p\geq 2.
This improve the known global regularity

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x},t)-e^{-t(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}\mathbf{u}_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in H^{1+\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})

shown in
[
10
,
11
]
.

(ii)

By developing the
L^{p}
-type elliptic regularity and “compactness lemma”, we firstly prove global regularity and decay behaviour of the forward self-similar solution for the critical problem where
\alpha=\frac{5}{6}
.

(iii)

Let us point out that we establish the optimal decay estimate (
1.22
) under the natural assumption
\sigma(\mathbf{x})\in C^{1,0}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, and we drop the additional condition in the existing literature, for example, that
\sigma(\mathbf{x})\in C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
with
\gamma>0
in
[
4
]
.

Notations.

We first agree that
\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle=(e+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
{B}_{r}(\mathbf{x}_{0})=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}|\,|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0}|<r_{2}\}
and

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{x}_{0};r_{1},r_{2})=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}|\,r_{1}<|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}_{0}|<r_{2}\}.

For
s\in\mathbf{R},
we denote
s=[s]+\{s\}
, where
[s]
is the integer part and
\{s\}
is the decimal part. Let
\mathscr{D}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right),\,\mathscr{S}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
be the space of smooth functions with compact support, the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing smooth functions and the space of tempered distributions on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
respectively.

The rest of our paper is organized as follows: In the next section, we give some preliminary
lemmas and
B^{2\alpha}_{p,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
-regularity of linearization to the Leray problem which is the key point in our proof. In Section
3
, we are devoted to proving the global regularity of weak solutions to the Leray problem.
Finally, the optimal decay estimates of weak solutions to Leray problem and the forward self-similar solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations are established in Section
4
.

2.
Preliminary and
L^{p}
-theory of linearization

2.1.
Littlewood-Paley theory

In this subsection, we will review some analysis statement including the Lttilewood-Paley theory, see for example
[
1
,
2
,
14
]
. First of all, let us recall Fourier transform that for each
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
,

\hat{f}(\xi):=\mathscr{F}(f)(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}e^{-i\mathbf{x}\cdot\xi}f(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{n},

and its inverse Fourier transform

\check{f}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathscr{F}^{-1}(f)(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n/2}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}e^{i\mathbf{x}\cdot\xi}f(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi,\quad\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{R}^{n}.

There exist two smooth radial functions
\hat{h}(\xi)\in\mathscr{D}\big(B_{\frac{4}{3}}(0)\big)
and
\hat{\varphi}(\xi)\in\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{C})
with
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(0;\frac{3}{4},\frac{8}{3})
such that
0\leqslant\hat{h}(\xi),\,\hat{\varphi}(\xi)\leqslant 1
and

\hat{h}(\xi)+\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\hat{\varphi}\left(2^{-j}\xi\right)=1,\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{n},

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\varphi}\left(2^{-j}\xi\right)=1,\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}.

Under the framework of dyadic partition of unity in frequency-space domain, we introduce some notations concerning the Littlewood-Paley theory.

•

Homogeneous decomposition: the dyadic blocks

\dot{\Delta}_{j}f(\mathbf{x})=2^{jn}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\varphi\left(2^{j}y\right)f(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},\quad\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{j}=\sum_{i=-1}^{1}\dot{\Delta}_{j+i},\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}

and the low-frequency cut-off operators

\dot{S}_{j}f(\mathbf{x})=2^{jn}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h\left(2^{j}\mathbf{y}\right)f(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}.

•

Inhomogeneous decomposition:

\Delta_{-1}f(\mathbf{x})=\dot{S}_{0}f(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}h\left(\mathbf{y}\right)f(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},

\Delta_{j}f(\mathbf{x})=\dot{\Delta}_{j}f(\mathbf{x})\quad\text{and}\quad S_{j}f(\mathbf{x})=\dot{S}_{j}f(\mathbf{x}),\quad\forall j\geqslant 0.

Let us remark that for each
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right),

S_{j}f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j^{\prime}\leqslant j-1}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}f(\mathbf{x}),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z},

but for each polynomial
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
whose compact support of
\hat{f}(\xi)
in
\{0\}
,

\dot{S}_{j}f(\mathbf{x})\neq\sum_{j^{\prime}\leqslant j-1}\dot{\Delta}_{j^{\prime}}f(\mathbf{x}),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}.

In order to remedy this defect, we introduce a new space of tempered distributions

\mathscr{S}_{h}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right):=\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)\backslash\mathscr{P},

which entails that for each
f\in\mathscr{S}_{h}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
, we have

\dot{S}_{j}f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j^{\prime}\leqslant j-1}\dot{\Delta}_{j^{\prime}}f(\mathbf{x}),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore, we can write the following Littlewood-Paley decompositions:

I_{d}=\sum_{j\geq-1}\Delta_{j}\quad\text{ and }\quad I_{d}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\Delta}_{j},

which make sense in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathscr{S}_{h}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
, respectively. Also, we note that the above dyadic block and low-frequency cut-off operator map
L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
into
L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
with norms independent of
j
and
p
, which is contained in the following Bernstein lemma.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
1
,
14
]
, Bernstein inequality)
.

Let
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
and
f\in L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
.
Then there exists a constant
C
such that for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\big\|D^{\alpha}\dot{S}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq C^{k}2^{j\left(k+n\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)}\big\|\dot{S}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)},

C^{-k}2^{jk}\big\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\big\|D^{\alpha}\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq C^{k}2^{j\left(k+n\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)}\big\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}.

Remark 2.2
.

The proof of the inequality (
2.1
) and the right inequality of (
2.1
) heavily relies on the Young inequality, that is,
\forall\,p,q,r\in[1,\infty],

(2.1)

\|f\ast g\|_{L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{q}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\|g\|_{L^{r}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)},

where
1+\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{r}.
This inequality could also be applied in the Lorentz spaces firstly developed by R. O’Neil in
[
17
]
, and a more general Young inequality in the Lorentz spaces such as in
[
7
]
, whose effect is obviously better than the classical Young inequality (
2.1
).

Lemma 2.3

(
[
7
]
, Theorem 1.2.13)
.

Let
1\leq\ell\leq s\leq\infty,\,1\leq r<\infty
and
1<p,q<\infty
with

1+\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{r},

If
f\in L^{q,\ell}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
and
g\in L^{r}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
,
then
f*g\in L^{p,s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
, and there exists a constant
C
depending only on
r,q,s
and
\ell
such that

\|f\ast g\|_{L^{p,s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{q,\ell}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\|g\|_{L^{r}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}.

Applying the above generalized Young inequality instead of (
2.1
) in the proof of the Bernstein inequality, we would have the generalized Bernstein inequality: Assume
1<p,q<\infty
and
p<q
, then

(2.2)

\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\big\|D^{\alpha}\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq C2^{j(k+\frac{n}{p})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\|_{L^{p,\infty}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)},

(2.3)

\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\big\|D^{\alpha}\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{q,1}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq C2^{j\left(k+n\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)}\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\|_{L^{p,\infty}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)},

so does the low-frequency cut-off operator
\dot{S}_{j}.

Next, we define the homogeneous Besov spaces in terms of the Bernstein inequality.

Definition 2.1

(Homogeneous Besov space)
.

Assume
s\in\mathbf{R},1\leqslant p,r\leqslant\infty
, one defines

\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})=\left\{f\in\mathscr{S}_{h}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)\,\big|\,\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}<\infty\right\},

where

\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}:=\left\|\left(2^{js}\big\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{p}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}.

Similar to the standard function spaces (e.g., Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
or
L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
spaces), the homogeneous Besov spaces have some properties illustrated by the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4
.

(i)

For each
p\geqslant 1
,
\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
with
s<\frac{n}{p}
or
\dot{B}_{p,1}^{{n}/{p}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
is Banach space.

(ii)

Inclusion: For each
0\leq r_{1}\leqslant r_{2}\leq\infty
,

\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r_{2}}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r_{1}}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}.

(iii)

Embedding: For each
1\leq p_{1}\leqslant p_{2}\leq\infty,

\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p_{2},r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p_{1},r}^{s+n\left(\frac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{1}{p_{2}}\right)}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}.

(iv)

The interpolation inequality: If
s_{2}\geq s_{1}
, there exists
\theta\in[0,1]
such that

\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r}^{\theta s_{1}+(1-\theta)s_{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}^{\theta}\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s_{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})\hskip 8.19447pt}^{1-\theta}.

(v)

The sharp interpolation inequality: If
s_{2}>s_{1}
, there exists
\theta\in(0,1)
such that

\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,1}^{\theta s_{1}+(1-\theta)s_{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant\frac{C}{s_{2}-s_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}+\frac{1}{1-{\theta}}\right)\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{s_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}^{\theta}\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{s_{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}^{1-\theta}.

(vi)

For
s\in\mathbf{R},

\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})=\dot{B}_{2,2}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n}),
and their norms are equivalent

\frac{1}{C^{|s|+1}}\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{2,2}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant C^{|s|+1}\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{2,2}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}.

Next, we will give the definition of the nonhomogeneous Besov spaces, which enjoys most properties which have been proven for the homogeneous Besov spaces.

Definition 2.2

(Inhomogeneous Besov space)
.

Let
s\in\mathbf{R},\,p,\,r\geqslant 1
, the inhomogeneous Besov space
B_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
defined as follows

B_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)=\left\{f(\mathbf{x})\mid f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)\text{ satisfies }\|f\|_{B_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}<\infty\right\},

where

\|f\|_{B_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}:=\left\|\left(2^{js}\left\|\Delta_{j}f\right\|_{p}\right)_{j\geq-1}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\})}.

Now we can state some useful properties of the nonhomogeneous Besov spaces.

Proposition 2.5
.

Assume
s\in\mathbf{R},1\leqslant p,r\leqslant\infty
, then there hold

(i)

(B_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n}),\|\cdot\|_{B_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})})
is the Banach space.

(ii)

The interpolation inequality: If
s_{1}<s_{2}
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for each
\theta\in(0,1),

\|f\|_{B_{p,1}^{\theta s_{1}+(1-\theta)s_{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leqslant\frac{C}{\left(s_{2}-s_{1}\right)\theta(1-\theta)}\|f\|_{B_{p,\infty}^{s_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}^{\theta}\|f\|_{B_{p,\infty}^{s_{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}^{1-\theta}.

(iii)

Density: For
p,r<\infty,

\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
is dense in
B_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right).

(iv)

Duality: For each
1\leqslant p,r<\infty,
we have
B_{p^{\prime},r^{\prime}}^{-s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)=\left(B_{p,r}^{s}\right)^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
.

(v)

For
\forall s>0
, we have from
[
2
, Chapter 6]
that

B_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)=L^{p}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)\cap\dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right).

(vi )

The general inclusions:

\displaystyle B_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow B_{p,r_{1}}^{s_{1}}(\mathbf{R}^{n}),\quad s_{1}<s\,\,\text{ or }\,\,s_{1}=s,\,r_{1}\geq r,

\displaystyle B_{p,r}^{s}(\mathbf{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow B_{p_{1},r}^{s-n\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)}(\mathbf{R}^{n}),\quad p_{1}\geq p,

\displaystyle B_{\infty,1}^{0}(\mathbf{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{n})=C(\mathbf{R}^{n})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{n}).

After stating the Besov type spaces, we will discuss the simpler Hölder spaces which are defined as follows.

Definition 2.3

(Hölder space
C^{k,\gamma}\left(\Omega\right)
)
.

Assume
k\in\mathbb{N},\,\gamma\in(0,1)
and
\Omega\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
. Let us define the Hölder space
C^{k,\gamma}\left(\Omega\right)
as follows:

C^{k,\gamma}\left(\Omega\right)=\left\{f(\mathbf{x})\in C_{b}^{k}\left(\Omega\right)\mid\|f\|_{C^{k,\gamma}(\Omega)}<\infty\right\},

where

\|f\|_{C^{k,\gamma}\left(\Omega\right)}=\|f\|_{C^{k}\left(\Omega\right)}+\sup_{\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}}\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\frac{\left|D^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x})-D^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{y})\right|}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{\gamma}}=:\|f\|_{C^{k}\left(\Omega\right)}+\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{k,\gamma}\left(\Omega\right)}.

Remark 2.6
.

Let us point out that if
\gamma=r-[r]>0
, we have

\frac{1}{C}\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{r}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leq\|f\|_{\dot{C}^{[r],\gamma}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}\leqslant C_{r}\|f\|_{\dot{B}_{\infty,\infty}^{r}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)},

where
C_{r}=C_{[r]}\big(\frac{1}{\gamma}-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\big).

Next, we introduce the definition of a weighted Hilbert space.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
s\geq 0
and
\textnormal{w}(\mathbf{x})
be the nonzero weighted function. Then we define the weighted space
H_{\rm w}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)
as

H_{\rm w}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right):=\left\{f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)\mid\|f\|_{H_{\rm w}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}<\infty\right\},

where

\|f\|_{H_{\rm w}^{s}\left(\mathbf{R}^{n}\right)}:=\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|f|^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{w}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}f|^{2}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{w}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Lastly we recall Bony’s paraproduct algorithm
[
3
]
, which is one of the most powerful tools of paradifferential calculus. In terms of the Littlewood-Paley decomposition,

f=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\quad\text{and}\quad g=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\Delta}_{j}g,

so that, we can write formally,

fg=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\dot{S}_{j+1}f\dot{S}_{j+1}g-\dot{S}_{j}f\dot{S}_{j}g\right).

After some simplifications, we readily get

\displaystyle fg

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\dot{S}_{j}g+\dot{\Delta}_{j}g\dot{S}_{j}f+\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\dot{\Delta}_{j}g\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\dot{S}_{j-1}g+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\Delta}_{j}g\dot{S}_{j-1}f+\sum_{\left|j-j^{\prime}\right|\leqslant 1}\dot{\Delta}_{j^{\prime}}f\dot{\Delta}_{j}g.

As a result, the product of two tempered distributions is decomposed into two paraproducts, respectively, the paraproduct term

\dot{T}_{f}g=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\dot{S}_{j-1}g

and the reminder term

\dot{R}(f,g)=\sum_{\left|j-j^{\prime}\right|\leqslant 1}\dot{\Delta}_{j^{\prime}}f\dot{\Delta}_{j}g=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{j}f\dot{\Delta}_{j}g.

In summary, the product of two tempered distributions can be split into three parts as follows

fg=\dot{T}_{f}g+\dot{T}_{g}f+\dot{R}(f,g).

2.2.
Some useful lemmas

In this subsection, we are devoted to showing some useful lemmas which play an important role in our proof. Let us begin with a compactness lemma which is a vital cornerstone for the critical case where
\alpha=\frac{5}{6}.

Lemma 2.7

(Compactness Lemma)
.

Assume the divergence free vector field
\mathbf{U}\in\dot{H}^{\frac{5}{6}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
and
\mathbf{V}\in{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
with
2\leq p<\frac{9}{2}
and
1\leq r\leq\infty
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0,

there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\varepsilon,p
and
\mathbf{U}
such that

(2.4)

\|\operatorname{div}\left(\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{V}\right)\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\varepsilon\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+C_{\varepsilon,\,p,\,\mathbf{U}}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Proof.

According to the Bony paraproduct decomposition, we split
\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{V}
into three parts as follows

\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{V}=\dot{T}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}+\dot{T}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{U}+\dot{R}\big(\mathbf{U},\,\mathbf{V}\big).

By the Hölder inequality, we can infer that for
2\leq p<\frac{9}{2},

(2.5)

\left\|\operatorname{div}\dot{T}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}.

Inserting

\mathbf{U}=\sum_{k<N}\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}+\sum_{k\geq N}\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}=:\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}+\mathbf{U}^{\natural},

into (
2.5
) gives

(2.6)

\begin{split}\left\|\operatorname{div}\dot{T}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq&C\left\|2^{k}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\\
&+C\left\|2^{k}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}.\end{split}

On one hand,

\displaystyle\left\|2^{k}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\leq

\displaystyle C\|\dot{S}_{N}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|2^{k}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C2^{\frac{2N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{1}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

By the sharp interpolation inequality, one has

\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{1}_{p,1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{2}{5}}_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{3}{5}}_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

We furthermore have by using Young’s inequality that

(2.7)

\begin{split}&\left\|2^{k}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\\
\leq&C2^{\frac{2N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{2}{5}}_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{3}{5}}_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
\leq&C_{\varepsilon}2^{\frac{5N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{5}{2}}_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

On the other hand,

(2.8)

\begin{split}\left\|2^{k}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\leq&C\left\|\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
\leq&C\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

Plugging (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) into (
2.6
), we obtain

(2.9)

\begin{split}\left\|\operatorname{div}\dot{T}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq&C\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
&+C_{\varepsilon}2^{\frac{5N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{5}{2}}_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

The divergence-free condition allows us to write

\operatorname{div}\dot{T}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{U}=\dot{T}_{\nabla\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{U}.

Moreover, we have by the Hölder inequality that

(2.10)

\begin{split}\left\|\operatorname{div}(\dot{T}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{U})\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq&C\left\|\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\\
&+C\left\|\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}.\end{split}

The Bernstein inequality in Lemma
2.1
can be applied to tackle the low-frequency part

(2.11)

\begin{split}\left\|\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\leq&C\|\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{\dot{B}^{1+\frac{3}{p}}_{p,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{-(1+\frac{3}{p})}_{\infty,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
\leq&C2^{\frac{5N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

By the Hölder inequality, we obtain

(2.12)

\begin{split}&\left\|\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\dot{S}_{k-1}\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}\\
\leq&C\|\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}+3\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}_{p,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\nabla\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{-\frac{5}{6}-3\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}_{\infty,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

Putting (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) into (
2.10
) to get

(2.13)

\begin{split}\left\|\operatorname{div}(\dot{T}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{U})\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq&C\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
&+C2^{\frac{5N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

For the remainder term, the Bernstein inequality in Lemma
2.1
and the Hölder inequality help us to conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\operatorname{div}\big(\dot{R}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})\big)\right\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left\|2^{\frac{5k}{2}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}^{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{k}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}+C\left\|2^{\frac{5k}{2}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}^{\natural}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{k}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\|_{\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C2^{\frac{5N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+C\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{V}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

This estimate together with (
2.9
) and (
2.13
), we immediately obtain that

(2.14)

\begin{split}\|\operatorname{div}\left(\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{V}\right)\|^{2}_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq&\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+C\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{3}}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
&+C_{\varepsilon}2^{\frac{5N}{3}}\|\mathbf{U}\|^{\frac{5}{2}}_{\dot{B}^{\frac{5}{6}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\|\mathbf{U}\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

Since
\ell^{r}(\mathbb{Z})\subset\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
for each
r\in[1,\infty)
and
\mathbf{U}\in\dot{H}^{\frac{5}{6}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
, we have that, for any
\varepsilon>0,
there exists a suitable integer
N
such that

\sup_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{6}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\bigg(\sum_{k\geq N}2^{\frac{5k}{3}}\|\dot{\Delta}_{k}\mathbf{U}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2C},

which together with (
2.14
) implies the desired result (
2.4
) in Lemma
2.7
.
∎

The action of the heat semigroup
e^{-t(-\Delta)^{\alpha}}
on distributions with Fourier transforms supported in an annulus is described in following lemma.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
p,\,r\in[1,\infty]
. Then there exists two positive constants
c
and
C
such that for
p\geq r,

(2.15)

\left\|\big(|\cdot|^{m}D^{\gamma}O_{\alpha,q}\big)\ast f\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C2^{q(|\gamma|-m+3(1/r-1/p))}e^{-ct2^{2q\alpha}}\big\|f\big\|_{L^{r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})},

where
G_{\alpha,q}
is the heat kernel with fractional diffusion
(-\Delta)^{\alpha}
and
O_{\alpha,q}=\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbb{P}G_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

First of all, we take the Fourier transform of
D^{\gamma}O_{\alpha,q}f
yielding

\mathscr{F}\left(D^{\gamma}O_{\alpha,q}f\right)=2^{q|\gamma|}\mathscr{F}\left(\mathbb{P}D^{\gamma}\varphi\right)(2^{q}\xi)e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\hat{f}.

Denoting
\phi(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{P}D^{\gamma}\varphi
, we easily verify that the
\phi(\mathbf{x})
is a smooth function with Fourier transform compactly supported in the ring
\mathscr{C}(0;3/4,8/3)
. Hence we have

(2.16)

D^{\gamma}O_{\alpha,q}f=2^{q|\gamma|}2^{3q}g\left(2^{2\alpha q}t,\,2^{q}\cdot\right)\ast f,

where

g(t,\mathbf{x})=\mathscr{F}^{-1}\left(\hat{\phi}(\xi)e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right).

When
m=2k_{0}
is nonnegative even number, we take the Fourier transform to obtain

\displaystyle|\mathbf{x}|^{m}g(t,\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{3}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}e^{i\mathbf{x}\cdot\xi}(I_{d}-\Delta_{\xi})^{3}(\Delta_{\xi})^{k_{0}}\left(\hat{\phi}(\xi)e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Thanks to the Leibiniz rule, one has

(\Delta_{\xi})^{k_{0}}\left(\hat{\phi}(\xi)e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i=0}^{m}C_{m}^{i}\left(\partial_{x_{j}}^{m-i}\hat{\phi}(\xi)\right)\left(\partial^{i}_{x_{j}}e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right).

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle(I_{d}-\Delta_{\xi})^{3}(\Delta_{\xi})^{k_{0}}\left(\hat{\phi}(\xi)e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta_{1}\leq\beta,\,|\beta|\leq 6}C_{\beta}^{\beta_{1}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i=0}^{m}C_{m}^{i}\partial^{\beta-\beta_{1}}\left(\partial_{x_{j}}^{m-i}\hat{\phi}(\xi)\right)\partial^{\beta_{1}}\left(\partial^{i}_{x_{j}}e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right)\bigg).

On the other hand, we have by the Faá-di-Bruno formula that

e^{t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}\left(\partial^{i}_{x_{j}}e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{11}+\cdots+\beta_{1\ell}=|\beta_{1}|\\
|\beta_{1k}|\geq 0\end{subarray}}(-t)^{|\beta_{1}|+i}\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}\partial^{\beta_{1k}}\partial^{i}_{x_{j}}\big(|\xi|^{2\alpha}\big).

Since
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}(\xi)\subset\mathscr{C}(0;3/4,8/3)
, we have that for each
\xi\in\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}(\xi),

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{\beta-\beta_{1}}\left(\partial_{x_{j}}^{m-i}\hat{\phi}(\xi)\right)\partial^{\beta_{1}}\left(\partial^{i}_{x_{j}}e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}\right)\right|\leq

\displaystyle C\left(1+t\right)^{|\beta_{1}|+i}e^{-t|\xi|^{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(1+t\right)^{|\beta_{1}|+i}e^{-\frac{9}{16}t}.

Since

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\left(1+t\right)^{6+m}e^{-\frac{9}{32}t}=0,

we immediately have the following pointwise estimate that for each even nonnegative integer
m=2k_{0}
,

(2.17)

|\mathbf{x}|^{m}g(t,\mathbf{x})\leq Ce^{-ct}\frac{1}{(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{3}}.

If the real number
m
is not an even integer, there exists
\theta\in(0,1)
such that

m=\theta\overline{m}+(1-\theta)\big(\overline{m}+2\big),

where
\overline{m}=2[m/2].

Furthermore, we get by the interpolation inequality and (
2.17
) that for each
m\geq 0,

|\mathbf{x}|^{m}g(t,\mathbf{x})=\left(|\mathbf{x}|^{\overline{m}}g(t,\mathbf{x})\right)^{\theta}\left(|\mathbf{x}|^{\overline{m}+2}g(t,\mathbf{x})\right)^{(1-\theta)}\leq Ce^{-ct}\frac{1}{(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{3}}.

From this estimate, it follows that

(2.18)

\big\||\cdot|^{m}g(t,\cdot)\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq Ce^{-\frac{9}{32}t}\quad\text{for each}\,\,p\in[1,\infty].

Recall from (
2.16
), one writes

\displaystyle\big(|\cdot|^{m}D^{\gamma}O_{\alpha,q}\big)\ast f=

\displaystyle\left(|\cdot|^{m}2^{q|\gamma|}2^{3q}g\left(2^{2\alpha q}t,\,2^{q}\cdot\right)\right)\ast f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{q(|\gamma|-m)}2^{3q}\left(|\cdot|^{m}g\right)\left(2^{2\alpha q}t,\,2^{q}\cdot\right)\ast f.

This equality together with (
2.18
) enables us to conclude that for each
1\leq r\leq p\leq\infty,

\left\|\big(|\cdot|^{m}D^{\gamma}O_{\alpha,q}\big)\ast f\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C2^{q(|\gamma|-m+3(1/r-1/p))}e^{-ct2^{2q\alpha}}\big\|f\big\|_{L^{r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

So we finish the proof of Lemma
2.8
.
∎

Lemma 2.9

(
[
5
]
, New Bernstein’s inequality)
.

Let
p\in[2,+\infty)
and
\alpha\in(0,1].
Then there exist two positive constants
c_{p}
and
C_{p}
such that for any
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

c_{p}2^{\frac{2\alpha j}{p}}\big\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\leq\left\|\Lambda^{\alpha}\left(\big|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big|^{\frac{p}{2}}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}^{\frac{2}{p}}\leq C_{p}2^{\frac{2\alpha j}{p}}\big\|\dot{\Delta}_{j}f\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}.

Lemma 2.10

(
[
11
]
)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1),\,f\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
and
\phi\in\dot{C}^{0,\beta}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\cap\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for each
\beta\in(0,\alpha),

\big\|\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},\,\phi\right]f\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\max\left\{\|\phi\|_{\dot{C}^{\beta}(\mathbf{R}^{3})},\,\|\phi\|_{\dot{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right\}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
,
\beta\in(0,1]
and
f\in L^{2}_{\langle\cdot\rangle^{2\beta}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
. Then we have

(i)

for each
\beta<s,

\left\|\left[\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta},\Lambda^{s}\right]f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})};

(ii)

for each
\beta\geq s,

\left\|\left[\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta},\Lambda^{s}\right]f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Proof.

If
\beta<s,
we immediately have by Lemma
2.10
that

\left\|\left[\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta},\Lambda^{s}\right]f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Now, it remains for us to consider the case where
\beta\geq s.

Since
\Lambda=\sqrt{-\Delta},
we have in terms of definition of fractional Laplacian that

\displaystyle\left[\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta},\Lambda^{s}\right]f(\mathbf{x})=-c_{s}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\frac{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{\beta}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s}}f(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

We decompose it into the following two parts

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\frac{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{\beta}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s}}f(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}\backslash B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}\right)\frac{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{\beta}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s}}f(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle{\rm I}+{\rm II}.

The first term
{\rm I}
can be bounded by

\displaystyle|{\rm I}|\leq\beta\int_{B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{2+s}}|f|(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},

which together with the Young inequality yields

(2.19)

\|{\rm I}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

As for term
{\rm II}
, we split it into two parts as follows

\displaystyle{\rm II}=

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)\backslash B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}+\int_{B^{c}_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)\backslash B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}\bigg)\frac{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{\beta}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s}}f(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle{\rm II}_{1}+{\rm II}_{2},

where
B^{c}_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)=\mathbf{R}^{3}\backslash B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0).

Since

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}-\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{\beta}\right|\leq\left||\mathbf{x}|^{2}-|\mathbf{y}|^{2}\right|^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\leq\left|\mathbf{x}\right|^{\beta}+\left|\mathbf{y}\right|^{\beta},

we see that

\displaystyle|{\rm II}_{2}|\leq\left(1+2^{\beta}\right)\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}\backslash B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s}}\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{\beta}|f|(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

Moreover, by the Young inequality again, we get

(2.20)

\|{\rm II}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Now, we turn to bound the term
{\rm II}_{1}.
For
\mathbf{y}\in B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)
, we observe by the triangle inequality that

(2.21)

\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{x}|\leq|\mathbf{x}|-|\mathbf{y}|\leq|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\leq|\mathbf{x}|+|\mathbf{y}|\leq\frac{3}{2}|\mathbf{x}|.

From this inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|{\rm II}_{1}\right|\leq 4\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}\backslash B_{100}(\mathbf{x})}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s-\beta}}|f|(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

By the Hölder inequality, the Young inequality and the fact
3+s-\beta>2
, one obtains

(2.22)

\left\|{\rm II}_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\left\|{\rm II}_{1}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

In terms of (
2.21
), we find that

\displaystyle\left|{\rm II}_{1}\right|\leq

\displaystyle 4\int_{B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{3+s-\beta}}|f|(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\leq\frac{C}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3+s-\beta}}\int_{B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)}|f|(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, one gets

\displaystyle\left|{\rm II}_{1}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3+s-\beta}}\int_{B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{y}|^{\beta}}\left(|\mathbf{y}|^{\beta}|f|\right)(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3+s-\beta}}\bigg(\int_{B_{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}(0)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{y}|^{2\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\frac{C}{|\mathbf{x}|^{\frac{3}{2}+s}}\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Hence, we have

(2.23)

\left\|{\rm II}_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3}\backslash B_{1}(0))}\leq C\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}\backslash B_{1}(0)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{x}|^{3+2s}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Collecting the above estimates (
2.19
), (
2.20
), (
2.22
) and (
2.23
), we eventually obtain

\left\|\left[\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta},\Lambda^{s}\right]f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})},

which implies the second desired estimate of Lemma
2.11
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1]
and
f\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that

(2.24)

\frac{1}{C}\big\|\langle\cdot\rangle\Lambda f\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\big\|\langle\cdot\rangle\nabla f\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\big\|\langle\cdot\rangle\Lambda f\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Proof.

According to the fact that
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{(-i\xi)(i\xi)}{|\xi|^{2}}=1
, we write

\Lambda f=-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathscr{R}_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}f,

where
\mathscr{R}_{i}
is the Riesz operator.

Recall from
[
18
, Chapter V-6.4]
that the function
|\mathbf{x}|^{a}
belong to the
A_{p}
class with
p>1,
if and only if
-n<a<n(p-1).

Thus, the function
|\mathbf{x}|^{2\beta}
belongs to the
A_{2}
class as long as
\beta\in(0,1]
, and then we immediately have by the weighted inequality for singular integral established in
[
18
, Chapter V-4.2]
that

(2.25)

\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\Lambda f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\mathscr{R}_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\partial_{x_{i}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})},

which gives the first inequality in (
2.24
).

To see the inverse inequality, we write
\partial_{x_{i}}f
in terms of Fourier transform as

\mathscr{F}(\partial_{x_{i}}f)(\xi)=-i\xi_{i}\hat{f}(\xi)=\frac{-i\xi_{i}}{|\xi|}|\xi|\hat{f}(\xi)=-\mathscr{F}\left(\mathscr{R}_{i}\Lambda f\right)(\xi)

for each
i\in\{1,2,3\}.

In parallel with (
2.25
), we conclude that for each
i\in\{1,2,3\},

\displaystyle\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\partial_{x_{i}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\mathscr{R}_{i}\Lambda f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\Lambda f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

The sum of all
i
is the second inequality in (
2.24
).
∎

2.3.
L^{p}
-type theory of the linearized Leray problem

In this subsection, we are going to investigate the high regularity of the Leray operator in
\mathbf{R}^{3}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{V}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}+\nabla P=\mathbf{F},\\
&\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{V}=0.\end{aligned}\ \right.

Let the couple
(\mathbf{V},P)\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times L^{\frac{3}{3-2\alpha}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
be a weak solution to the system (
2.26
) which satisfies

(2.27)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\frac{2-\alpha}{\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{V}\cdot\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{y}\cdot\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathbf{V}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\\
=&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}P\operatorname{div}\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{F}\cdot\phi\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla)\phi\cdot\mathbf{V}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\end{split}

for each divergence-free vector field
{\phi}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
s\in\mathbf{R},\,p\in[2,\infty),\,r\in[1,\infty]
and
\mathbf{U}\equiv 0
.
Assume that
(\mathbf{V},P)\in H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times L^{\frac{3}{3-2\alpha}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
is a weak solution to the system (
2.26
) with
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Then there exists a constant
C=C_{p,\alpha}>0
such that

(2.28)

\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s+2\alpha}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|P\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s+1}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C_{p,\alpha}\left(\left\|\mathbf{F}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right).

Proof.

Taking
\phi(\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{e}_{i}\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{y})
with
\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{y})=2^{3q}\varphi(2^{q}\mathbf{y})
in the above equality and denoting
f_{q}=\dot{\Delta}_{q}f
for each
f
, it yields by a simple calculation that for each
i=1,2,3,

(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}^{(i)}_{q}-\frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbf{V}^{(i)}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}^{(i)}_{q}+\partial_{x_{i}}P_{q}=\mathbf{F}^{(i)}_{q}+\mathbf{R}^{(i)}_{q}.

This implies that the couple
(\mathbf{V}_{q},P_{q}):=(\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbf{V},\dot{\Delta}_{q}P)
is smooth and solves the following system in the whole space

(2.29)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{q}-\frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{q}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}_{q}+\nabla P_{q}=\mathbf{F}_{q}+\mathbf{R}_{q},\\
&\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{V}_{q}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where the commutator
R_{q}
defined by

R_{q}=[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}=\dot{\Delta}_{q}\left(\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{V}\right)-\mathbf{x}\otimes\dot{\Delta}_{q}\mathbf{V}.

Since the Fourier transform
\mathscr{F}:\,\mathscr{S}\left(\mathbf{R}^{3}\right)\to\mathscr{S}\left(\mathbf{R}^{3}\right)
is a bounded linear operator, we know that each term in (
2.29
) is a Schwarz function.
So, multiplying the first equation of (
2.29
) by
|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}
with
p\geq 2
and then integrating the resulting equality in space variable over the whole space, we get

(2.30)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p}\\
&-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
=&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(-\nabla P_{q}+\mathbf{F}_{q}\right)\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{R}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

The new Bernstein inequality in Lemma
2.9
enables us to infer the lower bound of the first quantity in the left side of equality (
2.30
) that for each
p\in[2,\infty),

(2.31)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(-\Delta)^{\alpha}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq c_{p}2^{2q\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Integrating by parts yields

(2.32)

\begin{split}&-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
=&-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p}+\frac{3}{2p\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p}=\frac{3+p-2p\alpha}{2p\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p}.\end{split}

By the Hölder inequality, one has

(2.33)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(-\nabla P_{q}+\mathbf{F}_{q}\right)\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\left(\left\|\nabla P_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\mathbf{F}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right)\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p-1}.

Now we turn to deal with the integral term involving commutator which takes

\displaystyle[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\mathbf{y}\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\otimes\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\varphi_{q}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.

Letting
\bar{\varphi}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}\varphi(\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{R}^{3}),
taking the Fourier transform and using the support property of
\varphi
, we find

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\left([\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}\right)(\xi)=

\displaystyle 2^{-q}\widehat{\bar{\varphi}_{q}}(\xi)\widehat{\mathbf{V}}(\xi)=i2^{-q}\operatorname{div}_{\xi}\varphi\left({2^{-q}}{\xi}\right)\otimes\widehat{\mathbf{V}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i2^{-q}\operatorname{div}_{\xi}\varphi\left({2^{-q}}{\xi}\right)\otimes\widehat{\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}}(\xi),

which means that

(2.34)

[\dot{\Delta}_{q},\mathbf{x}\otimes]\mathbf{V}=2^{-q}\bar{\varphi}_{q}\ast\left(\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right)(\mathbf{x}).

With the equality (
2.34
) in hand, we infer by the Hölder inequality and the Bernstein inequality in Lemma
2.1
that

(2.35)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{R}_{q}\,|\mathbf{V}_{q}|^{p-2}\mathbf{V}_{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq&\frac{1}{2\alpha}2^{-q}\left\|\bar{h}_{q}\ast\nabla\left(\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right)\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p-1}\\
\leq&\frac{C}{2\alpha}\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{p-1}.\end{split}

Inserting estimates (
2.31
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
) and (
2.35
) into the equality (
2.30
) leads to

(2.36)

\begin{split}&c_{p}2^{2q\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{3+p-2p\alpha}{2p\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
\leq&\left\|\nabla P_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\mathbf{F}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{2\alpha}\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

Our task is now to bound the quantity concerning the pressure
P.
Applying the divergence operator to the first equation of (
2.29
), we readily obtain by a simple calculation that

(2.37)

-\Delta P_{q}=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F}_{q}\quad\text{in }\mathbf{R}^{3}.

The standard
L^{p}
-estimate for elliptic equations for each
p\in[2,\infty),

\left\|P_{q}\right\|_{\dot{W}^{2,p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{F}_{q}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}

means that

(2.38)

2^{q}\left\|P_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\|\mathbf{F}_{q}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\quad\text{for each}\,\,p\in[2,\infty).

Plugging estimate (
2.38
) into (
2.36
), we immediately have

(2.39)

\begin{split}&c_{p}2^{2q\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{3+p-2p\alpha}{2p\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+2^{q}\left\|P_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
\leq&C\left\|\mathbf{F}_{q}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{2\alpha}\left\|\widetilde{\dot{\Delta}}_{q}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.\end{split}

Multiplying (
2.39
) by
2^{qs}
and then taking the
\ell^{r}
-norm of the resulting inequality with respect to
q
, we eventually obtain that for each
p\in[2,\infty),

\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s+2\alpha}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|P\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s+1}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C_{p,\alpha}\left(\left\|\mathbf{F}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\right),

which implies the desired estimate in Theorem
2.13
.
∎

Next, we give the estimate of weak solution
\mathbf{V}
in the weighted Hilbert space. In forthcoming part of this subsection, we always assume that

\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}_{1}+\Div\mathbf{F}_{2}.

Theorem 2.14
.

Assume the divergence free vector field
\mathbf{U}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\cap\dot{W}^{1,\frac{3}{\alpha}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
.
Let the couple
(\mathbf{V},P)\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\times H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
be a weak solution to the system (
2.26
) with
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(i)

if
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\mathbf{F}_{1}\in L_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
and
\mathbf{F}_{2}\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
satisfying

\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\Div\mathbf{F}_{2}\right\|_{H^{-\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}<\infty,

we have that for each
\beta\in(0,\alpha),

(2.40)

\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\Big(\left\|\mathbf{F}_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\Div\mathbf{F}_{2}\right\|_{H^{-\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\|\mathbf{F}_{2}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\Big);

(ii)

if
\alpha=1,

\mathbf{F}_{1}\in L_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2}}^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
and
\mathbf{F}_{2}\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
satisfying

\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\Div\mathbf{F}_{2}\right\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}<\infty,

we have

(2.41)

\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle\mathbf{V}\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\leq C\Big(\left\|\mathbf{F}_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2}}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle\Div\mathbf{F}_{2}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\|\mathbf{F}_{2}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\Big).

Proof.

Since the proof of (
2.41
) was shown in
[
11
]
, so we focus on the proof of the case where
\alpha\in(0,1).

Denoting
g_{R}(\mathbf{x})=g(\mathbf{x}/R)
with
g(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{(1+{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}})^{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\beta}{2}}}
and
R\gg 1
,

\mathbf{V}_{R}:=g_{R}\mathbf{V},\quad P_{R}:=g_{R}P\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{F}_{R}:=g_{R}\mathbf{F},

we easily find by taking the test function as
\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}g_{R}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{V}_{R}
in (
2.27
) that

(2.42)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\left(g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\frac{2-\alpha}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\\
&-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}\left(g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}\left(g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
=&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{F}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}_{R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\nabla P\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\end{split}

where and what in follows, we denote
f_{\beta,R}(\mathbf{x})=g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}f(\mathbf{x}).

Firstly, we rewrite the first term in the left side of the above equality as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\left(g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left(g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\right)\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\right]\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{2}+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\right]\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\right]\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
2.10
, we find that for each
\beta<\alpha,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\right]\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\right]\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\Lambda^{\alpha}\left(\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{R}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Also, we have that for each
\beta<\alpha,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{\beta}\right]\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\Lambda^{\alpha},g_{R}\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\right]\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left\|\mathbf{V}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Thus, we have

(2.43)

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}\cdot\Lambda^{\alpha}\left(g_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}_{R}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq\left\|\Lambda^{\alpha}\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}^{2}-C\|\mathbf{V}\|_{H^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|_{{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Noting that

\displaystyle-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}-\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\left|\mathbf{V}\right|^{2}\left(g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

one has by the integrating by parts that

\displaystyle-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5-4\alpha}{4\alpha}\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}|\mathbf{V}_{R}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}|\mathbf{V}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

A simple calculation yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}^{2}_{R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2(\beta-1)}\mathbf{V}^{2}_{R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}-\frac{e\beta}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2(\beta-1)}\mathbf{V}^{2}_{R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1+2\beta}{4\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\frac{\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{R^{2}}}{1+\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{R^{2}}}\mathbf{V}^{2}_{\beta,R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq-\frac{1+2\beta}{4\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}.

Therefore, we have

(2.44)

\begin{split}&-\frac{2\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\left\|\mathbf{V}_{\beta,R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}\varphi^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\\
\geq&\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{\beta}\mathbf{V}_{R}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})}-\frac{e\beta}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2(\beta-1)}\mathbf{V}^{2}_{R}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

Integrating by parts, one obtains

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{3}}\mathbf{U}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{V}\left(g^{2}_{R}\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2\beta}\mathbf{V}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=
